\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\Omega^{n+1}_{\EuScript}{X}(\log\EuScript{P}_{\mathrm{red}})\big([(\alpha+n-p+1)\EuScript{P}]\big)\Big\}[-p]

and, for
k
such that
1<k\leqslant n-p+2
,

\Phi_{k}(p):\Big\{\Phi_{1}^{<n-k+2}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}F_{0}\mathscr{O}_{\EuScript}{X}(*\EuScript{P}_{\mathrm{red}})\Omega_{\EuScript}{X}^{n-k+2}\big([(\alpha+n-p-k+2)\EuScript{P}]\big)\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\cdots\\
\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\Big(\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}F_{j}\mathscr{O}_{\EuScript}{X}(*\EuScript{P}_{\mathrm{red}})v^{j}\Big)\Omega_{\EuScript}{X}^{n+1}\big([(\alpha+n-p+1)\EuScript{P}]\big)\Big\}.

Then
\Phi_{1}(p)=F_{\mathrm{NP},\alpha}^{p}\DR\EuScript{E}
and
\Phi_{n-p+2}(p)=F_{\alpha}^{p}\DR\EuScript{E}
. On each successive quotient
\gr_{k}^{\Phi}=\Phi_{k}/\Phi_{k-1}
, the induced differential becomes
-(v/x^{\boldsymbol{e}})\sum e_{i}{\mathrm{d}}x_{i}/x_{i}
. Except at the first non-zero term, the complex
\gr_{k}^{\Phi}
decomposes into many parts of the Koszul complex associated with
-(v/x^{\boldsymbol{e}})\{e_{1}{\mathrm{d}}x_{1}/x_{1},\dots,e_{\ell}{\mathrm{d}}x_{\ell}/x_{\ell}\}
. By a direct computation, the first non-zero chain map of
\gr_{k}^{\Phi}
is injective. In particular,
\gr_{k}^{\Phi}
is quasi-isomorphic to zero.
∎

Let us now end the direct proof of (
9.16
). In the discussion of the toric case in
[
Yu14
,
§
4]
, a specific resolution
\pi:\widetilde{\EuScript}{X}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\EuScript{X}
of
vf
is constructed inductively by taking blowups along irreducible components of the intersection of the pole set
\EuScript{P}_{\mathrm{red}}
of
vf
with its zero set
(f^{-1}(0)\times\mathbb{P}^{1}_{v})\cup(X\times\{v=0\})
. Then it is shown in loc. cit. that (
9.16
) holds when we replace its left-hand side with
F_{{\mathrm{N}},\alpha}^{p}\DR\EuScript{E}
. Lemma
9.17
allows us to conclude.∎

Appendix. Brieskorn lattices and Hodge filtration

A.d.
Brieskorn lattices in dimension one

Let
(M,F_{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}M)
be a holonomic
\mathbb{C}[t]\langle\partial_{t}\rangle
-module equipped with a good filtration. We denote by
G
the holonomic
\mathbb{C}[t]\langle\partial_{t}\rangle
-module
\mathbb{C}[\partial_{t},\partial_{t}^{-1}]\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[\partial_{t}]}M
. If we identify
\mathbb{C}[t]\langle\partial_{t}\rangle
with
\mathbb{C}[v]\langle\partial_{v}\rangle
by the Laplace correspondence
t\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}\partial_{v}
,
\partial_{t}\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}-v
, we also regard
G
as a holonomic
\mathbb{C}[v]\langle\partial_{v}\rangle
-module on which the multiplication by
v
is bijective. It is therefore also a
\mathbb{C}[v,v^{-1}]
-module. We will denote by
\widehat{\mathrm{loc}}
the natural morphism
M\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}G
.

The Brieskorn lattice
G_{0}^{(F_{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet})}
of the filtration
F_{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}M
is defined as the saturation of the filtration by the operator
\partial_{t}^{-1}
, that is,

G_{0}^{(F_{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet})}:=\sum_{j}\partial_{t}^{-j}\widehat{\mathrm{loc}}(F_{j}M)\subset G.

It is naturally a
\mathbb{C}[\partial_{t}^{-1}]
-module (equivalently, a
\mathbb{C}[v^{-1}]
-module). We will also set
G^{p}_{(F_{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet})}=v^{-p}G_{0}^{(F_{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet})}
for any
p\in\mathbb{Z}
. Let us make the link with the definition in
[
Sab08
,
§
1.d]
. Let
p_{o}
be an index of generation, so that
F_{p_{o}+\ell}M=F_{p_{o}}M+\cdots+\partial_{t}^{\ell}F_{p_{o}}M
for any
\ell\geqslant 0
. Then the definition in loc. cit. is

G_{0}^{(F_{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet})}=\partial_{t}^{-p_{o}}\sum_{j\geqslant 0}\partial_{t}^{-j}\widehat{\mathrm{loc}}(F_{p_{o}}M).

Let us check that both definitions give the same result. Let us write
(*)
as

G_{0}^{(F_{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet})}=\partial_{t}^{-p_{o}}\sum_{j}\partial_{t}^{-j}\widehat{\mathrm{loc}}(F_{p_{o}+j}M).

Firstly, for
j\leqslant 0
, we have

\partial_{t}^{-j}\widehat{\mathrm{loc}}(F_{p_{o}+j}M)=\widehat{\mathrm{loc}}(\partial_{t}^{-j}F_{p_{o}+j}M)\subset\widehat{\mathrm{loc}}(F_{p_{o}}M),

so we can also write

\displaystyle G_{0}^{(F_{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet})}

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}^{-p_{o}}\sum_{j\geqslant 0}\partial_{t}^{-j}\widehat{\mathrm{loc}}(F_{p_{o}+j}M)

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}^{-p_{o}}\sum_{j\geqslant 0}\partial_{t}^{-j}\big[\widehat{\mathrm{loc}}(F_{p_{o}}M)+\cdots+\partial_{t}^{j}\widehat{\mathrm{loc}}(F_{p_{o}}M)\big]

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}^{-p_{o}}\sum_{j\geqslant 0}\partial_{t}^{-j}\widehat{\mathrm{loc}}(F_{p_{o}}M)=(**).

We now express the Brieskorn lattice of the filtration as obtained by a push-forward operation. We consider the holonomic
\mathbb{C}[t,v]\langle\partial_{t},\partial_{v}\rangle
-module
M[v,v^{-1}]\mathrm{e}^{vt}
. The
(t,v)
-Brieskorn lattice is the
\mathbb{C}[t,v^{-1}]
-module defined by the following formula
(see
[
Sab99
,
§
1]
)
:

\mathscr{G}_{0}(M,F_{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}M)=\mathop{\textstyle\bigoplus}\displaylimits_{j}F_{j}M\cdot v^{-j}\mathrm{e}^{vt}\subset M[v,v^{-1}]\mathrm{e}^{vt},\quad\mathscr{G}^{p}(M,F_{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}M)=v^{-p}\mathscr{G}_{0}(M,F_{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}M).

We have
\partial_{t}\mathscr{G}^{p}(M,F_{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}M)\!\subset\!\mathscr{G}^{p-1}(M,F_{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}M)
since
\partial_{t}F_{j}M\!\subset\!F_{j+1}M
and
\partial_{t}\mathrm{e}^{vt}\!=\!v\mathrm{e}^{vt}
. The relative de Rham complex

\DR(M[v,v^{-1}]\mathrm{e}^{vt}):=\Big\{M[v,v^{-1}]\mathrm{e}^{vt}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow{\textstyle\kern 4.0pt\partial_{t}\kern 3.0pt}}{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\partial_{t}}}{{\longrightarrow}}}{}{}M[v,v^{-1}]\mathrm{e}^{vt}\Big\}

has cohomology in degree one only, and we have a natural identification as
\mathbb{C}[v]\langle\partial_{v}\rangle
-modules

\coker\Big[\partial_{t}:M[v,v^{-1}]\mathrm{e}^{vt}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}M[v,v^{-1}]\mathrm{e}^{vt}\Big]\simeq G

by sending
\sum_{j}m_{j}v^{-j}\mathrm{e}^{vt}
to
\sum_{j}(-\partial_{t})^{-j}\widehat{\mathrm{loc}}(m_{j})
. This relative de Rham complex is filtered by the subcomplexes

\mathscr{G}^{p}\DR(M[v,v^{-1}]\mathrm{e}^{vt}):=\Big\{\mathscr{G}^{p}(M,F_{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}M)\mathchoice{\xrightarrow{\textstyle\kern 4.0pt\partial_{t}\kern 3.0pt}}{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\partial_{t}}}{{\longrightarrow}}}{}{}\mathscr{G}^{p-1}(M,F_{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}M)\Big\}.

(A.18)

Lemma A.19

(Push-forward)
\pointrait

The relative de Rham complex is strictly filtered by the
\mathscr{G}^{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}
-filtration and, through the previous identification, the filtration on its
H^{1}\simeq G
is equal to
G^{{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}-1}_{(F_{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet})}
.

Proof.

With respect to the previous identification,
\mathscr{G}^{p}(M,F_{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}M)
is sent onto
G^{p}_{(F_{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet})}
according to the definition
(*)
. It remains to show that
(\image\partial_{t})\cap\mathscr{G}^{p}(M,F_{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}M)=\partial_{t}\mathscr{G}^{p+1}(M,F_{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}M)
and it is enough to check this for
p=0
.

We have
\partial_{t}\big(\sum_{j}m_{j}v^{-j}\mathrm{e}^{vt}\big)=\sum_{j}(\partial_{t}m_{j}+m_{j+1})v^{-j}\mathrm{e}^{vt}
and by induction on
j
we deduce that
(\partial_{t}m_{j}+m_{j+1})\in\ F_{j}M
for all
j
implies
m_{j}\in F_{j-1}M
for all
j
.
∎

Remark A.20

(Rees modules)
.

It will also be useful to have the following interpretation in terms of Rees modules (see Proof of Theorem
4.1
), for which we use the variable
u
instead of
z
here. We can twist the Rees module
R_{F}M
by
\mathrm{e}^{t/u}
by changing the action of
u\partial_{t}
to that of
u\partial_{t}+1
. We denote the corresponding
R_{F}\mathbb{C}[t]\langle\partial_{t}\rangle
-module by
R_{F}M\cdot\mathrm{e}^{t/u}
. This is nothing but
\mathscr{G}_{0}(M,F_{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}M)
by the change of variable
u=v^{-1}
.

The push-forward of an
R_{F}\mathbb{C}[t]\langle\partial_{t}\rangle
-module
\mathscr{M}
by the constant map
q:\mathbb{C}_{t}=\Spec\mathbb{C}[t]\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\Spec\mathbb{C}
is nothing but the de Rham complex
\partial_{t}:\mathscr{M}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}u^{-1}\mathscr{M}
, where the latter term is in degree zero. The push-forward
q_{+}(R_{F}M\cdot\mathrm{e}^{t/u})
is thus equal to the complex (with the
{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}
in degree zero):

R_{F}M\cdot\mathrm{e}^{t/u}\mathchoice{\xrightarrow{\textstyle\kern 4.0pt\partial_{t}\kern 3.0pt}}{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\partial_{t}}}{{\longrightarrow}}}{}{}\underset{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}{u^{-1}R_{F}M\cdot\mathrm{e}^{t/u}}.

Setting
u=v^{-1}
we thus have an identification

\textstyle{\mathscr{G}_{0}(M,F_{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\partial_{t}}
\textstyle{\mathscr{G}^{-1}(M,F_{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{R_{F}M\cdot\mathrm{e}^{t/u}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\partial_{t}}
\textstyle{u^{-1}R_{F}M\cdot\mathrm{e}^{t/u}}

so we can interpret
G^{-1}_{(F_{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet})}
as
H^{0}q_{+}(R_{F}M\cdot\mathrm{e}^{t/u})
, while
H^{-1}q_{+}(R_{F}M\cdot\mathrm{e}^{t/u})=0
.

A.e.
Brieskorn lattices in arbitrary dimension

We fix
k
and we will apply the previous result to
(M,F_{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}M)=\mathscr{H}^{k-\dim X}f_{+}(\mathscr{O}_{X}(*D),(F_{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}\mathscr{O}_{X}(*H))(*P_{\mathrm{red}}))
. Here, we identify filtered
\mathbb{C}[t]\langle\partial_{t}\rangle
-modules and
\mathscr{D}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(*\infty)
-modules filtered by
\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(*\infty)
-modules. We know that the latter underlies a mixed Hodge module (up to a shift of the filtration), according to
[
Sai90
]
. Working with Rees modules, the strictness property for the push-forward
f_{+}
of mixed Hodge modules can also be stated by saying that the push-forward
f_{+}\big[(R_{F}\mathscr{O}_{X}(*H))(*P_{\mathrm{red}})\big]
is strict, and thus

\mathscr{H}^{k-\dim X}f_{+}\big((R_{F}\mathscr{O}_{X}(*H))(*P_{\mathrm{red}})\big)=R_{F}M.

On the other hand, one checks that

\mathscr{H}^{k-\dim X}f_{+}\big((R_{F}\mathscr{O}_{X}(*H))(*P_{\mathrm{red}})\cdot\mathrm{e}^{f/u}\big)\simeq\big(\mathscr{H}^{k-\dim X}f_{+}(R_{F}\mathscr{O}_{X}(*H)(*P_{\mathrm{red}})\big)\cdot\mathrm{e}^{t/u},

and since
\mathscr{H}^{j}q_{+}(R_{F}M\cdot\mathrm{e}^{t/u})=0
for
j\neq 0
, we conclude

\mathscr{H}^{k-\dim X}(q\circ f)_{+}\big((R_{F}\mathscr{O}_{X}(*H))(*P_{\mathrm{red}})\cdot\mathrm{e}^{f/u}\big)\simeq\mathscr{H}^{0}q_{+}(R_{F}M\cdot\mathrm{e}^{t/u}).

The left-hand term is by definition equal to

\boldsymbol{H}^{k}\big(X,(\Omega_{X}^{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{X}}\!\!(u^{-{\raisebox{1.0pt}{${\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}$}}}R_{F}\mathscr{O}_{X}(*H))(*P_{\mathrm{red}}),{\mathrm{d}}+u^{-1}{\mathrm{d}}f)\big),

that is, to
G_{0}\boldsymbol{H}^{k}_{u}
as defined by (
8.12
), while the right-hand term is equal to
G^{-1}
as defined above.
